Their strike range S stays 145 (B10). `tilapia-0` loops over the root tangle (x 465 to 685, y 330 to 500: the middle and floor bands). `tilapia-1` loops on a slant from (2522, 256) and (2532, 256) down to (2502, 480) and (2495, 480), beside the spots past the current, under the top-right bank (surface and middle). `tilapia-2` loops tall and narrow between the bottom-row nets and the main path's dive (x 1265 to 1270, y 330 to 600: middle and floor). `tilapia-3` loops at the right edge of the top right (x 2823 to 2840, y 256 to 430: surface and middle). `tilapia-1` and `tilapia-3` reach the surface band, each more than 205 pixels from every Common spot. Every two tilapia loops are more than twice the lunge range of 145 pixels apart. Four common carp, `carp-0` to `carp-3`, loop tall and narrow, with the carp's own tuning and its chase default: sight 220 pixels, leash 120, and speed 115 pixels per second. Their S is 120. `carp-0` (x 1550 to 1560) is under the main path's dive, `carp-1` (x 1801 to 1806) beyond it, and `carp-3` (x 2224 to 2229) in the current, each from y 444 to 600, the middle band down to the floor. `carp-2` (x 2003 to 2008, y 150 to 306) is in the top row of the gap screen, from the surface band down to the middle, so its reach covers the top row under the banks beside it. Each carp loop is farther than its lunge plus the Area 4 lunge, 265 pixels, from every tilapia loop, and more than twice its lunge, 240 pixels, from every other carp loop. Each carp lap is no longer than the longest tilapia lap, `tilapia-0`'s 15.6 seconds, so the fairness start window stays the longest tilapia loop. Only one predator warns or strikes at a time (A1). Two punting poles stand in top-row screens with no garden plot, 2 seconds out of step, and both moved: `chinampa-1/punting-pole-1` to x 324, offset 0, 100 pixels deep (it was x 420, 180 deep), in the first screen just past the Home Spot's column, clear of `tilapia-0`, its danger zone down to y 198; and `chinampa-1/punting-pole-2` to x 2190, offset 2 seconds, 180 pixels deep (it was x 2075, 350 deep), in the gap screen between `carp-2` and the top-right bank, its danger zone down to y 278. Each pole's sweep is farther than every predator's lunge from that predator's loop, each pole's cycle is no longer than the longest tilapia lap, the two danger zones never overlap, and the exit is outside both. The ghost nets moved to make room. Ghost nets 1 and 2, in the bottom row, are at x 1005 to 1065 (x 905 to 965 before issue #134), at the same heights, y 400 to 470 and 570 to 640. They drift 120 pixels left, with the current's flow, in step on the 16 second cycle, so each drift path is x 885 to 1065. The bottom-row way between them stays 100 pixels tall, a 64 pixel gap. Nets 3 and 4, with the main path's dive between them, moved up to y 170 to 270 (from 260 to 360), at x 1460 to 1510 and 1610 to 1660, and the main path's narrowest gap stays 64 pixels, between them. Net 5 moved from the way into the top-right bank (x 2200 to 2260) to the surface beyond the dive, x 1745 to 1805, at the same height, y 94 to 170. Nets 3 to 5 stay still. Each net, by its whole drift path, is more than a lunge plus its reach and the safe passing width from every tilapia and carp loop, and at least 190.5 pixels from the current. One stranding place moved: `strand-4`, from (1800, 250) to (1700, 380), below the top of the dive between `carp-0` and `carp-1`. No hiding spot moved. It has 28 snacks: 8 worms, 6 bugs, 7 tadpoles, and 7 freshwater shrimp, so half are mobile. At least 4 snacks start in the current, 4 placed there on purpose, every one a worm or a bug, a floor (open question 302). The tadpoles swim at 60 pixels per second (issue #131) on wander loops placed after the predators, poles, and nets (issue #134), four in the top row and three in the bottom row: through (350, 110), (470, 100), (690, 118), (610, 255), and (400, 240); (800, 110), (980, 100), (1140, 130), (1130, 290), (950, 300), and (830, 240); (1060, 110), (1220, 100), (1380, 120), (1300, 180), (1160, 250), and (1060, 230); (2215, 110), (2400, 100), (2535, 105), (2420, 180), and (2260, 250); (302, 503), (310, 650), (450, 654), (624, 652), and (470, 560); (1745, 315), (1850, 300), (1962, 457), (2095, 500), (1935, 580), (2095, 645), and (1900, 470); and (2400, 648), (2560, 655), (2770, 640), (2690, 500), and (2540, 580). Each loop has 4 to 8 turn points, uneven legs of 90 to 300 pixels, and a lap of at most 40 seconds, and tadpole n starts (n − 1) sevenths of its lap ahead. Each home is 320 to 480 pixels wide and 150 to 360 tall, at least 3 are in each screen row, no two home centres are closer than 240 pixels, none is in the current, and each is at least 80 pixels from every ghost net's whole drift path. A one-leg axolotl can catch every tadpole (the one-leg catch proof). The shrimp hop about homes of 3 or 4 hop spots, each home in a box at most 200 x 160 pixels, four in the top row and three in the bottom row: (390, 120), (470, 110), (520, 160), and (430, 170); (1150, 112), (1230, 108), (1290, 150), and (1200, 160); (2265, 120), (2335, 112), (2380, 165), and (2300, 175); (2600, 110), (2680, 105), (2760, 120), and (2700, 135); (2430, 630), (2500, 645), and (2570, 640); (1950, 460), (2030, 450), (2070, 560), and (1990, 600); and (2650, 620), (2720, 645), (2800, 610), and (2740, 590). They keep a 60 pixel trigger, a 1.4 second rest, and a 52 pixel flick at 130 pixels per second, with Area 4's hop row (Tuning values). Every hop spot has a clear flick target in at least one of 8 directions, every loop and home passes its clear check on every seed, and the snack motion rules hold (H4). The shrimp replace the one-time darters, so Area 4 has none. The Snack magnet pulls at 72 pixels per second, 1.2 times the tadpole speed (H4; open question 362). Its layout test proves the bar and its snack ramp row (open questions 336 and 395), and its golden was re-recorded on purpose (open questions 338 and 398).

        Area 5, danger pass: built, issue #118 (named defaults ND-1 to ND-16, open questions 303 to 318; owner decision in session, 2026-10-24, option A for ND-4; starting values, to tune in play-testing), and brought up to the whole-water danger bar by issue #135 (named defaults ND-1 to ND-9, open questions 382 to 390; owner decisions on issue #135, 2026-10-24 and 2026-10-25). Area 5 meets the full danger bar with the whole-water test on the default seed, and every other seed builds the same threats and water (open questions 305, 311, and 387). It has 16 moving threats for its 14 screens: 3 tilapia, 3 garter snakes, 5 common carp, and 5 punting poles, so 3 predator species and 1 moving hazard type. Every predator chases. Measured: the longest quiet swim anywhere in the water is 457.23 pixels, from (1744, 406) to (1416, 94), under the 480 limit; the floor lane's longest quiet stretch is 235 pixels; every depth is patrolled, each tilapia and carp loop reaching 24 pixels or more into two of the three depth bands and each species reaching all three; there is danger at the surface; and its 5 carp meet the carp target for 14 screens (open question 387). Along the main path alone, issue #118 measured a longest quiet stretch of 458.8 pixels (open question 311). The tilapia play Area 5's own tilapia tuning, one more step up, with a 0.825 second warning (open question 304) and the full-bar chase, `TILAPIA_CHASE`: sight 240 pixels, leash 160, speed 125 pixels per second. The garter snakes play `CHINAMPA_2_SNAKE`, the garter snake with `GARTER_SNAKE_CHASE`: sight 200, leash 0, speed 120, so each slides only along its stretch, keeping its warn on route rule and its depth bounds (open question 303). The carp play `CHINAMPA_2_CARP`, one step up the predator ladder from Area 4's carp: a 0.95 second warning, over the 0.8 second floor, a tail sweep at 210 pixels per second for 0.6 seconds, a reach of 125 pixels, a 2.3 second cooldown, a 38 pixel per second patrol, and the carp's chase default, `CARP_CHASE`: sight 220 pixels, leash 120, speed 115 pixels per second (open question 383). Only one of its predators warns or strikes at a time (A1). The tilapia loops (open question 305): `tilapia-0` x 2160 to 2250 and y 330 to 620, beside the root tangle, from the bottom-row path down to the spots past the current; `tilapia-1` x 2767 to 2773 and y 250 to 306, just past the current, from the surface band into the middle one, under the second snake's stretch; and `tilapia-2` x 3316 to 3320 and y 444 to 650, up and down the right edge of the bottom row. Issue #135 moved `tilapia-1` and `tilapia-2` and made them taller, so each reaches into two depth bands (open question 384): before it, `tilapia-1` looped x 3140 to 3250 and y 280 to 320 in the top right, and `tilapia-2`, new in issue #118, x 3140 to 3250 and y 625 to 650, low in the bottom right corner. The bar holds with 3 tilapia, so there is no fourth. The snakes (open question 310), all at y 100 in open top-row water, with the deep lane below y 260 under each: `garter-snake-0` x 1080 to 1240, `garter-snake-1` x 2700 to 2860, and `garter-snake-2`, new, x 2525 to 2685. The carp (open question 383), each loop reaching into two depth bands: `carp-0` x 341 to 345 and y 380 to 560, up and down the bottom row beside the exit; `carp-1` x 830 to 832 and y 320 to 500, between the first two poles; `carp-2` from under the first snake down to the bed and along it under the first channel's mats, through (1096, 362), (1265, 362), (1265, 560), (1320, 648), (1700, 648), and (1096, 648); `carp-3` x 2527 to 2528 and y 444 to 650, beside the gap current; and `carp-4` x 3049 to 3078 and y 250 to 306, from the surface band into the middle one, under the bank in the top right. Each carp loop is more than 275 pixels from every tilapia loop, 250 from every other carp loop, and 235 from every snake stretch, and `carp-0` and `carp-2` patrol the bottom row west of x 1850. The punting poles (open question 308), in top-row columns with no garden plot (x, depth, offset): `chinampa-2/punting-pole-1` at x 476, 440 pixels, 0 seconds; `chinampa-2/punting-pole-2` at x 964, 400 pixels, 2 seconds; `chinampa-2/punting-pole-3` at x 1400, 360 pixels, 0 seconds; `chinampa-2/punting-pole-4` at x 1760, 340 pixels, 2 seconds; and `chinampa-2/punting-pole-5` at x 1840, 340 pixels, 0 seconds. Each jab crosses the main path in both rows, each sweep is farther than every predator's strike reach from its loop, 150 pixels for the tilapia, 110 for the snakes, and 125 for the carp (the strike-only pole rule since issue #135, open question 382; issue #118 also kept each sweep farther than the strike reach plus the chase leash, and two touch zones still came inside a predator's reach, open question 308), no two pole touch zones overlap, each stays at least 36 pixels from every mat sweep, out of the safe area, every bank overhang, and the exit, and neighbours are 2 seconds out of step. Each pole deeper than the 180 pixel default draws its whole shaft up to its boat (open question 306). Its 5 ghost nets stay still. Issue #118 kept its mats exactly as built (open question 309). To make room for the carp, issue #135 moved ghost nets 2 to 4 and mat pair 2 within the bottom row, keeping their counts and what each pair gates (owner decision on issue #135, 2026-10-25): nets 2 and 3 from x 1080 to 1140 to x 1917 to 1955, past the last pole with the main path between them, at y 362 to 400 and 484 to 544 (from 372 to 420 and 504 to 570); net 4, under the first mat pair, from y 446 to 516 to y 446 to 468, low and flat; and mat pair 2 from x 580 to 730 to x 535 to 685. Net 1, in the top row over mat pair 2, moved with it, from x 600 to 700 to x 555 to 635. The main path's bottom row now passes between nets 2 and 3, through (2010, 400), (1960, 442), (1910, 442), and (1860, 410), before the first channel's mats. Every carp loop is more than its strike plus 18 pixels, 143 pixels, from each mat sweep, because the carp's mat rule is strike only (owner decision on issue #135, 2026-10-25; open question 382); the tilapia keep 150 + 160 + 18 = 328 pixels and the snakes 172. It has 32 snacks: 7 worms, 6 bugs, 10 tadpoles at 70 pixels per second (issue #131) on wander loops of 4 to 6 points since issue #135, six in the top row and four in the bottom row, each round open water beside the threats, each loop a tenth of its own lap, 11.8 to 14.7 seconds, further on than the last (open questions 362 and 386), and 9 freshwater shrimp, each on a home of 3 hop spots since issue #135, five in the top row and four in the bottom row, each starting on a spot out of every threat's reach, with a 60 pixel trigger, a 1.2 second rest, and a 60 pixel flick at 145 pixels per second, hopping between their spots (open question 386; the loops and homes are in Tuning values). So 19 are mobile (59%), Area 5's snack ramp row. At least 4 snacks start in the current, 4 placed there on purpose, all worms or bugs, a floor (open question 302). The freshwater shrimp replace the 3 one-time darters, so Area 5 has none. The Snack magnet pulls at 84 pixels per second, 1.2 times the tadpole speed (H4; open questions 314 and 362). To meet the bar within Area 5's rules, these moved (open question 316): the main path's fourth point from (1000, 300) to (1100, 300); the Rare spots `chinampa-2/spot-bank-7` from (873, 110) to (2375, 110), `chinampa-2/spot-bank-9` from (2911, 110) to (2980, 110), and `chinampa-2/spot-bank-10` from (2967, 110) to (3030, 138); the stranding places `chinampa-2/strand-3` from (1820, 310) to (1980, 300), `chinampa-2/strand-4` from (2200, 280) to (2200, 230), and `chinampa-2/strand-6` from (3200, 560) to (3200, 470); `garter-snake-0` from x 1040 to 1240; `garter-snake-1` from x 2600 to 2800; and `tilapia-0` from x 2160 to 2260 and y 560 to 620. For issue #135 these moved too (open questions 384 and 385): `tilapia-1` and `tilapia-2`, ghost nets 1 to 4, mat pair 2, and the main path's bottom row (above); and the Rare spots `chinampa-2/spot-bank-9` to `chinampa-2/spot-bank-14`, under the bank in the top right, from (2980, 110), (3030, 138), (3080, 110), (3140, 110), (3200, 110), and (3260, 110) to (2971, 138), (3013, 100), (3069, 100), (3125, 100), (3167, 138), and (3223, 138), each within the second snake's or `carp-4`'s strike band. The snakes, mat pair 1, the poles, and the stranding places stayed where they were.
    - Area shape rule (owner decision on epic #32, 2026-10-22): no two areas in a row share the same overall flow. Area 4 is down and right: it starts in the top left, and its exit is deep in the bottom right (issue #76). The first area that needs a shape the area framework cannot express (the Home Spot not on the left, the exit not on the right, or more than 2 rows) generalises the framework in its own PR, with an ADR. Area 5 is a U-turn: it starts in the top left, runs right along the top row, goes down in the rightmost screen column, and comes back left along the bottom row to its exit under the Home Spot (issue #103; open question 225). It is the first area whose exit is not on the right, so it generalised the framework, with [ADR 0020](../adr/0020-u-turn-areas-garter-snake-and-hyacinth-mats.md): the owner moved that generalisation into issue #103's PR instead of its own PR (owner comment on issue #103, 2026-10-22 19:58; open question 246). Still assuming a direction: the stream mouth rule, for looks with a stream, and areas of more than 2 rows, which are untested (ADR 0020).
    - Unlocking: beating area N unlocks area N+1. There are no star gates, so stars stay a bonus. This replaces "some areas may need stars to unlock". Area 1 is always unlocked. An area is beaten once its exit star (Star 1, H6) is saved, so the unlocked areas come from the saved stars, and no list of unlocked areas is saved (open question 111).
    - Area map (built, issue #73; habitat panels and area names, issue #113): Go! on the explorer card (F7) opens the area map, "Where to?" (open question 116). It always shows after Go!, even with only Area 1, and it shows only the areas in the area data, with no placeholders for areas still to come (open question 114). In a dev build, the dev-only `?area` flag skips it (open question 124).
        - Look: the areas sit on a winding path, grouped by habitat. Each run of a habitat's areas sits on its own rounded panel, tinted a light shade of its marker colour, with an ink outline (open questions 282 and 285). A folder tab on the panel's top edge names the habitat and its areas: "Canal: Area 1", "Lily pad pond: Areas 2 and 3", and "Chinampa gardens: Areas 4 and 5", with the numbers from the area data (issue #113; open question 284). The panels sit behind the path, and the tabs and their labels on top. The groups never read by colour alone (X2): the panel outline, the tab label, and the marker shape carry them. A locked habitat keeps its name and its panel, its markers keep the locked look, and the map shows no "???" (open question 279). Each area marker shows its number and its best stars (0 to 3). A locked area also shows a padlock badge. The lily pad pond has its own section, and its markers are notched lily pads, green when unlocked and white when locked (issue #75). The chinampa gardens have their own section too, and their markers are garden plots, a wide rounded rectangle with roots hanging from its lower edge, marigold orange when unlocked and white when locked (issue #76; open question 206). The chinampa gardens section holds Areas 4 and 5 (issue #103). The newest unlocked area shows a "New!" pill while it has no stars (open question 113).
        - Choosing: pressing an unlocked area starts the trip there, so any unlocked area can be replayed (H7). The area chosen is saved as the last area (D1, D2). Pressing a locked area does not start a trip. It shows "Beat Area N to unlock!" for 3 seconds, where N is the area before it (open question 115).
        - Back: it returns to the explorer card, with the same explorer chosen and Go! focused (F7).
        - Keys: focus starts on the last area played, else on the newest unlocked area (open question 112). Tab and Shift+Tab move through the markers, then Back. The arrow keys move along the path. Enter or Space presses the focused control, ignoring key repeat. Escape is Back. The focused control has a double focus ring.
        - Scrolling: the map scrolls by drag and by mouse wheel, and follows the focus.
        - Reduced motion: the path does not draw in, scrolling is instant, and the "New!" pill is still.
        - Fit: text, markers, stars, and Back never shrink. Each tab label wraps inside its panel's width, and a panel grows to hold its tab. The fit order in the [art style guide](../art-style-guide.md) (section 8) still applies: when the cells do not fit, the tabs leave their band, and each sits inside its panel at the start of its run, along the scroll axis, 12 pixels before its first cell. On a portrait canvas, where the map scrolls down, each tab sits on its panel's top edge, the panels are full width, there is no band, and that step is never taken (open question 280). The map fits 460 x 320, 320 x 440, 375 x 667, 844 x 390, and 1280 x 800, with Areas 1 to 4 and with all five areas (open question 281).
    - Refuge (built, issue #74; [ADR 0017](../adr/0017-xochimilco-refuge-and-habitat-milestones.md)): each beaten area restores part of Xochimilco and clears its trash from a safe axolotl refuge, based on real conservation work there. The Refuge tile in the Home Tank opens it (C7). The refuge keeps growing as areas and habitats are added, and it has no end: each habitat that becomes clean is a habitat milestone, and the game stays playable. There is no "refuge complete" ending or finale (owner decision on issue #74, which replaces the area 10 ending).
        - Progress: it comes from the saved stars alone, so nothing new is saved (D2). The refuge has one section per habitat that holds at least one area, in area-table order. Each area has one batch of trash in its habitat's section, cleared once the area is beaten (its exit star saved). A section is hidden, shown as "???" with "Beat Area N" for its first area, until any of its areas is beaten. It is clean once every area in it is beaten. A partly cleaned section stays tinted, with its remaining trash. A dim, dashed "More to come" section ends the refuge. A new habitat in the area data adds a section with no other change. So the chinampa gardens get their own section, with Area 4's trash batch, shown as "???" with "Beat Area 4" until Area 4 is beaten; beating it cleans the section and shows its habitat milestone, naming "the chinampa gardens" (issue #76; [ADR 0017](../adr/0017-xochimilco-refuge-and-habitat-milestones.md)). Area 5 adds a second batch to the chinampa gardens section, which is now clean only once both Area 4 and Area 5 are beaten (issue #103). So a save that had already cleaned it by beating Area 4 sees it partly cleaned again, with Area 5's trash, has no "See it again" there until Area 5 is beaten, and gets "All clean!" again when Area 5 is beaten. No progress is lost and the save does not change (open question 242; [ADR 0020](../adr/0020-u-turn-areas-garter-snake-and-hyacinth-mats.md)).
        - Clean-up: a trip that newly beats an area adds a pending clean-up. When the refuge next opens, it plays every pending clean-up together, after a 0.6 second hold: the cleared trash floats up and fades with a sparkle, and a section that is now clean crossfades from its dirty picture to its clean one as its plants grow. Under reduced motion each step is only a 0.2 second fade (open question 185).
        - Nudge: while a clean-up is pending, the Refuge tile shows a "Refuge grew" badge: a 22 pixel pink disc with an ink ring and an ink star, which reads without colour (X2).
        - Habitat milestone: a trip that newly makes a section clean. Back in the Home Tank, after the tank arrival card and its first-find cards (C15) and before the hub takes input, a milestone card shows for each newly clean habitat, one at a time, in habitat order. After the Paint shell card's "Try it now!", the milestone cards show first, then restyle opens (open question 180). The card has the title "All clean!" (open question 179), the clean section's picture, the body "You and {name} cleaned up the {habitat}! Real people in Xochimilco do this work too.", with the habitat name in lower case and {name} the trip's explorer, and one "Keep exploring" button, which ignores presses for the first 0.8 seconds. Its title hides where it does not fit: at 460 to 1024 wide by 320, 340, and 360 tall. Closest fits: 5 pixels to spare with the title hidden at 320 tall (padding 12); with the title, 0.5 pixels at 320 to 375 x 480 (padding 24) and 20.5 pixels at 320 x 440. "See it again" on a clean section shows its card again, naming the current explorer (open question 181).
        - Pending rules: pending clean-ups and milestones are kept for this play session only and never saved. A dev area trip never counts. Start over clears them. Quitting before the refuge opens loses only the animation and the card; the section is still cleaned. Replaying a beaten area changes nothing. A save that already cleaned a section gets no catch-up card, but "See it again" works.
        - Screen: the title "Xochimilco refuge" shows only where it fits. Sections are 200 pixels wide, with a 200 x 96 picture, side by side, 12 pixels apart; two whole sections show at 460 x 320. Under its name, each section that shows has an area line naming its areas, "Area 1", "Areas 2 and 3", or "Areas 4 and 5", in the same words as the area map's tabs, with the numbers from the area data; a "???" section shows none (issue #113; open question 286). The area line sits in its own row under the name row. On the 320 and 340 pixel tall canvases, where that row would push the sections past the screen, it sits right under the name instead, over the top of the picture, which the refuge art keeps clear for it. The "More to come" label stays level with the section names. Back (200 x 64) is at the top left. The Previous and Next tiles, each with its arrow and its label (Previous 118 x 64, Next 65 x 64), are at the top right when the canvas is at least 439 pixels wide, else in a bottom row (open question 184). It pans by drag (from 8 pixels, following the pointer, with no fling), the mouse wheel, Previous and Next, and the Left and Right keys. Tab moves through the "See it again" tiles, Previous, Next, and Back. Escape is Back. Back returns to the Home Tank with Refuge focused.
        - Fact cards: "Axolotl refuges" and "Canal clean-ups" (C14).
3. **N3 Hazards and predators** (issues #32 and #73). A hazard is defined in the glossary. A predator is a kind of hazard: an animal that hunts and strikes, like the tilapia (the glossary's "Predator" row, issue #73). The N2 area table says which area brings each new hazard, predator, and timing or puzzle element. The first time the player meets a new hazard or predator, its fact card appears, like A21.
    - Built in the area framework (issue #73). The plastic worm, in Area 2, is the first new hazard to ship (issue #75). The heron, in Area 3, is the first new predator to ship (issue #98). Since issue #111 it is a wading stalker that warns on sight, and Area 3 has no timing element. The ghost net, in Area 4, is the second new hazard to ship, and Area 4's tilapia are the first predators played with an area's own tuning, the predator ladder's first step (issue #76; N2). The garter snake, in Area 5, is the third predator species, and the water hyacinth mats, in Area 5 too, are the first timing element to ship and the first thing that blocks the explorer (issue #103; [ADR 0020](../adr/0020-u-turn-areas-garter-snake-and-hyacinth-mats.md)). The danger pass's shared parts, the chase rule, the moving hazard type with the punting pole, the common carp, and drifting ghost nets, are built in issue #117, opt-in; the phase 2 issues add them to areas, and each area's own text says which it has (N2, the danger pass; [ADR 0021](../adr/0021-danger-pass-chase-rule-and-moving-hazards.md)).
        - Shared predator rules: every predator patrols, warns, strikes, and cools down by the same rules, with its own species data: speed, patrol, warning wind-up, strike range, speed, and length, cooldown, hit, touch, and body reach, bump, meet range, and fact card. A species may add a rule for when it may warn. A species may also be marked as never touching, so it never bumps (open question 170). The tilapia is the first species and plays exactly as before. Every predator warns on sight: its warning may start only when the axolotl is within its strike range of where the predator is, outside the safe area, allowed by the species' rule, and no other predator is warning or striking. At the end of the warning the same check runs again: if it fails, the warning is cancelled and the cooldown runs.
        - Shared rule options (issue #111): a species may turn on any of these, and the tilapia uses none of them, so it plays exactly as before:
            - Sight cover: it hunts by sight and cannot see an axolotl in cover: inside a reed patch, under a lily pad, or in the cave. Past the current is open water, not cover. It does not warn at an axolotl in cover, the end-of-warning check cancels a warning whose target is in cover, and its strike never connects with an axolotl in cover (open question 207).
            - Fixed strike spot: the strike spot is fixed where the axolotl is at the warning start, instead of the aim being locked at the strike start. The predator stays where it is through the warning and the strike, and its strike is a stab whose tip moves from the predator to that spot. If the axolotl has only moved by the end of the warning, the stab still goes to the fixed spot and misses (open question 210).
            - Still cooldown: through the cooldown, the predator stands still facing the axolotl instead of swimming its route (open question 213).
            - Warn on route (issue #103; owner decision on issue #103, 2026-10-23, option 1, comment 6371850978; open question 247): the predator warns only while it is on its route, within 2 pixels of it as a closed loop, inclusive. Off its route, for example after a strike carried it away, it does not warn, and the end-of-warning check cancels a warning; it swims its route through the cooldown and patrol, and may warn again once back on it. Its movement and its strike do not change. The garter snake uses it.
        - One at a time: only one predator warns or strikes at a time, across every predator in the area, whatever its species (A1; open question 118). Chasing is not attacking, so any number of predators may chase at once, and a moving hazard is outside this rule.
        - Chase rule (issue #117; owner decision on issue #117, 2026-10-23; [ADR 0021](../adr/0021-danger-pass-chase-rule-and-moving-hazards.md); open questions 249 to 252): on sight, a predator leaves its route and follows the axolotl on a leash, then returns to its route. It is opt-in: only a predator whose tuning has a chase (its sight, leash, and speed) chases, and every predator without one plays step for step as before. An area's tilapia chase through its own tilapia tuning, and any other species through its own tuning for that area, keeping its id.
            - When: only on patrol. If the predator may warn (A1, A3), it warns as before. Otherwise, when the axolotl is exposed (outside the safe area, and, for a species with sight cover, not in cover) and within its sight of the predator, it swims toward the axolotl at its chase speed. It closes no nearer than its lunge range: there it warns, or, while another predator is warning or striking (A1), it waits where it is.
            - Leash: it never ends a step more than its leash from its patrol loop; a step that would go farther ends on the leash's edge. Out of sight, beyond its leash, or with the axolotl in the safe area, it swims its route at patrol speed, so it goes back to it. The bounds and the safe area keep-out apply to every move, so it never enters the Home Spot safe area, and where they conflict it holds its place. It faces the way it moves.
            - The warning, the strike, and the cooldown do not change; in the cooldown it swims its route, as before. A species with the warn on route rule, the garter snake, chases along its route only: its leash counts as 0, so the snake slides along its stretch and its depth bounds hold.
            - Limits (open question 250): the chase speed is strictly between a 2-leg axolotl's top speed (112.5 pixels per second) and a 4-leg axolotl's (150), so a careless player gets caught but a skilled one escapes, and Tail Dash (330 pixels per second) always outruns a chaser; the sight is greater than the lunge range of the tuning that carries the chase; and the leash is 0 or more. A chase on a species with a fixed strike spot or a still cooldown, the heron, is refused. The heron does not chase; it keeps its wading stalk.
            - No new cue, and reduced motion does not change the chase. The risk ladder (B10) and the gap measure ignore the chase: a chasing predator's strike range S stays its lunge range from its patrol loop (open question 252). Only the danger bar counts the leash (N2).
        - Predator hits: every predator's hit costs one leg and starts protected time (A5), and a Shell perk can block it (A22).
        - Hazards: each hazard is data: whether it costs a leg or only slows, its shape (a circle or a rectangle), its reach from the shape's edge, its meet range, and its fact card. Touching a hazard that costs a leg is a hit, by the same rules as a predator's hit (A5, A11, A22), and leaves the bump cooldown as it was (open question 126). A hazard that only slows never hits.
        - Timing elements: each one is data with a cycle: how long one cycle lasts, and how long it is active in each cycle. An element type may also carry, as optional data, a fact card and a meet range, and a mat pair's timing; a spawn may carry a mat pair's geometry (issue #103). Elements without them play exactly as before.
        - Fact cards: each hazard and predator has its own fact card and meet range in its data, and so may each timing element type (issue #103; open question 229). The first time the axolotl comes within that range, outside the Home Spot, its card shows, like A21, and it is found in the Collection Book (C14). An element is met within its meet range of its sweep, the whole space its moving parts can take. The tilapia card, its text, its 180 pixel meet range, and its timing are unchanged.
        - Risk: every predator and every hazard that costs a leg counts for the risk ladder (B10; open question 120).
    - Guardrails:
        - Every hazard and predator is gentle and never scary, and every hit is avoidable with skill.
        - Every predator gives a visible warning before every strike, like the tilapia's telegraph before its lunge.
        - A predator's strike that connects is a hit, and A4, A5, A11, and A22 apply to it as they do to a tilapia's hit.
        - Timing and puzzle elements stay simple for ages 8 to 12. A stuck player gets a gentle hint, and every area can always be finished (issue #77).
    - Predators:
        - Tilapia: as in the MVP (the A rows). From Area 4 on, each area plays its tilapia one step up the predator ladder (N2), with its own tuning as area data; an area with none plays the MVP tuning. In Area 4 (built, issue #76; provisional): a 0.85 second warning, strike speed 400 pixels per second, strike reach (lunge range) 145 pixels, a 2.2 second cooldown, and patrol speed 50 pixels per second, and, since issue #119, the full-bar chase (sight 240 pixels, leash 160, speed 125 pixels per second). The 0.8 second floor, the 0.4 second strike, and every other tilapia value stay the same. Areas 1 to 3 are unchanged (open question 192).
        - Heron (built, issue #98; a wading stalker since issue #111, owner decisions 2026-10-22 and 2026-10-23; values in Tuning values, provisional): it watches from above the water and stabs near the surface ("stay deep, or hide"). Its warning starts on sight, like the tilapia's, so the player dodges by swimming away during the warning or by staying deep or in cover. It uses all three shared rule options above. The heron's step on the epic #32 predator ladder is set by its warning time, stab reach, and cooldown: Area 3's step is 1.2 seconds, 110 pixels, and 3 seconds. A later area with a heron tightens it, and the 0.8 second warning floor (A2) always holds (owner comment on issue #32, 2026-10-23).
            - Where: two herons stand in the back half of Area 3, with their legs in the water and their head and neck above the surface. Each wades slowly back and forth along its own short stretch, the line where its beak rests, at y 120 (N2). The herons are always shown.
            - Reach: a heron reaches 110 pixels from where it stands on its stretch, so it warns at no axolotl deeper than y 230, and its stab hits no axolotl centred below about y 256. A heron never warns at an axolotl in the bottom band, below y 230 down to the sea bed, or in the safe area (open question 208).
            - Cover: a heron hunts by sight and cannot see an axolotl inside a reed patch, under a lily pad, or in the root arch. Past the current is open water, not cover. It does not warn at an axolotl in cover, a warning is cancelled if its target goes into cover, and a stab never connects with an axolotl in cover. The tilapia ignores cover, as before. Treasure spots may still sit within a heron's reach (B10; owner decision on issue #111, 2026-10-23; open question 207). This replaces "No cover shields the axolotl: only depth does."
            - Warning, 1.2 seconds: it starts on sight when the axolotl is within the heron's reach, outside the safe area, not in cover, and no other predator is warning or striking (A1). It is longer than the tilapia's telegraph and above the 0.8 second floor (A2). The heron freezes and draws its neck back into an S, and its head keeps following the axolotl (open question 209).
            - Strike, 0.3 seconds: a fast stab with the beak closed, at the spot where the axolotl was when the warning started. At the end of the warning, if the axolotl is out of reach, in the safe area, or in cover, the warning is cancelled and the cooldown runs; if it has only moved, the stab still goes to the fixed spot and misses. So swimming away during the warning dodges it (open questions 210 and 211).
            - Hit: a stab whose tip comes within 26 pixels of the axolotl, the tilapia's Medium hit reach, outside the safe area and not in cover, is one hit (A4, A5, A11, A22), with the usual bump cooldown. The heron is never shown grabbing, holding, lifting, or eating the axolotl: a hit shows only the usual leg-loss poof ([ADR 0007](../adr/0007-kid-safety-and-privacy.md)). It never touches or bumps the axolotl (open question 170).
            - Cooldown, 3 seconds: after a stab, hit or miss, or a cancelled warning, it stands still with its beak back at rest, then wades on (open question 213).
            - Warning cue: the S-neck pose is the warning, with no "!" bubble and no glint. In greyscale the S-neck silhouette carries the cue. Under reduced motion every change is a pose switch with no tween; the heron still wades, but its legs do not step (open question 212).
            - Head: it follows the axolotl only while the axolotl is within 180 pixels and not in cover; otherwise the heron looks along its wading direction (open question 218).
            - One at a time: only one predator warns or strikes at a time, across both herons, the 4 tilapia, and, since issue #133, both carp (A1; open question 118).
            - Fact card: "Meet the heron" (C13), found the first time the axolotl comes within 180 pixels of a heron, outside the Home Spot. Cover does not stop a meeting (open question 216).
        - Garter snake (built, issue #103; owner decisions on issue #103, 2026-10-22 and 2026-10-23; values in Tuning values, provisional): a friendly, round-headed, striped snake that swims slowly along a short stretch at the surface in a gentle S-wiggle ("stay deep"). It plays the tilapia's shared rules with its own species data, `garter-snake`, and of the shared rule options it uses only warn on route: no sight cover, no fixed strike spot, and no still cooldown. So cover does not hide the axolotl from it; only distance and depth keep the axolotl safe, as with the tilapia (open question 231).
            - Where: three snakes in Area 5 since issue #118 (open question 310), `garter-snake-0` on a stretch from x 1080 to 1240, `garter-snake-1` from x 2700 to 2860, and `garter-snake-2` from x 2525 to 2685, all at y 100, the line its head swims along, each in open top-row water (issue #103 built two, from x 1040 to 1240 and 2600 to 2800). Since issue #118 each chases along its stretch, with `GARTER_SNAKE_CHASE` (the chase rule above; open question 303). The snake's position is its head: touch, hit, and reach are measured from it. Its drawn body trails behind along the stretch and is not a touch area. The S-wiggle is art only (open question 230).
            - Warning, 0.8 seconds, the floor (A2): it starts by distance, like the tilapia's, when the axolotl is within 110 pixels of the snake's head, outside the safe area, the snake is on its stretch (warn on route), and no other predator is warning or striking (A1). The snake stops, and ripple rings spread from its head.
            - Strike: a quick dart along the aim locked at the end of the warning, at 360 pixels per second for 0.35 seconds, 126 pixels. A dart that connects within 26 pixels, the tilapia's Medium hit reach, is one hit, costing a leg (A4, A5, A11, A22). The dart has no depth cap.
            - Cooldown, 2 seconds: it swims its route, back to its stretch, and patrols at 55 pixels per second. It warns again only once its head is back within 2 pixels of its stretch.
            - Depth: with the head line at y 100, the 2 pixel route tolerance, the 110 pixel reach, the 126 pixel dart, and the tilapia's hit reach 26 and touch reach 29, it warns at no axolotl deeper than y 212, hits none deeper than y 254, its head goes no deeper than y 228, and the water at y 260 and below is safe from it. So the bottom row is out of its reach, and under each stretch a deep lane below y 260 is out of every snake's reach. Since issue #135, tilapia and carp patrol the deep lane too, so it is safe from the snakes only (open question 385). Measured on Area 5's real routes over 60 seconds, with a swimmer diving under each snake, swimming near the surface, and staying in the deep lane: the deepest head was at y 227.96, the deepest warning target at y 206.96, and the deep lane had no events (open questions 230 and 247).
            - Touch: touching a snake that is not darting gives the tilapia's gentle bump and costs no leg, with the tilapia's hit, touch, and body reach and bump.
            - Warning cue: the ripple rings only, with no "!" bubble and no glint, as for the heron (open question 160). Under reduced motion the rings show still and fade, and the snake still swims its stretch but holds one S pose. In greyscale the rings have an outline (open question 231).
            - Never scary: its mouth is closed and it has no fangs. It is never shown biting, holding, or eating the axolotl; a hit shows only the usual leg-loss poof ([ADR 0007](../adr/0007-kid-safety-and-privacy.md)).
            - One at a time: only one predator warns or strikes at a time, across all three snakes and all three tilapia (A1).
            - Fact card: "Meet garter snakes" (C13), found the first time the axolotl comes within 180 pixels of a snake's head, outside the Home Spot.
        - Common carp (built as a shared part, issue #117, opt-in per area; owner decision on issue #117, 2026-10-23; values in Tuning values, provisional; open question 253): a real invasive fish in Xochimilco, a friendly, round, bronze-gold carp with two short barbels. It roots slowly along the bed and sweeps a wide, slow reach. It plays the tilapia's shared rules with its own species data, `carp`, and no rule options. It is a danger part (N2, the danger pass), so it is outside the one-new-thing rule.
            - Where: it swims at every depth, surface, middle and floor, on the patrol routes a phase 2 area gives it (N2, the depth rule), and digs in the mud on the bed (issue #130; open questions 347 and 352).
            - Warning, 1 second: it tips its nose down and puffs a small mud cloud. In greyscale the puff has an ink outline. Under reduced motion the puff shows still and fades, and the carp holds one pose.
            - Strike: a slow, wide tail sweep along the aim locked at the end of the warning, 200 pixels per second for 0.6 seconds, 120 pixels. A sweep that connects within 38 pixels, its hit reach, is one hit, costing a leg (A4, A5, A11, A22). Its reach (lunge range) is 120 pixels, so S = 120 for the risk ladder (B10).
            - Touch: touching a carp that is not sweeping gives the tilapia's gentle bump and costs no leg.
            - Chase: its default chase has a sight of 220 pixels, a leash of 120, and a speed of 115 pixels per second (the chase rule above; open question 251).
            - Never scary: its mouth is closed and it has no teeth. It is never shown biting or holding the axolotl; a hit shows only the usual leg-loss poof ([ADR 0007](../adr/0007-kid-safety-and-privacy.md)).
            - Fact card: "Meet the carp" (C13), found the first time the axolotl comes within 180 pixels of a carp, outside the Home Spot.
        - Crayfish: it patrols the rocky bottom slowly, and its claws open before it pinches.
        - Black bass: it is big and fast, with a long lunge and a clear warning first.
    - Hazards:
        - Plastic worms (built, issue #75; values in Tuning values, provisional): plastic bits that look like worms. Eating one makes the axolotl queasy and slow for a moment. It only slows: it never hits, and it is not a threat for the risk ladder (B10).
            - Look: loose, worm-shaped plastic only, with no other litter and no fishing line. The tell is shiny, too-bright colour (neon pink or blue with a white glint) and a stiff, straight rod shape with no wiggle; real worms keep their wiggle and their wavy shape. In greyscale the stiff straight rod and the white glint tell it apart.
            - Touch: the axolotl eats one when its head touches it, with the same head reach as a snack (A19).
            - Eating one: the axolotl spits it out with a little "bleh", its cheeks tint green, and it wobbles. It never looks scary or sick ([ADR 0007](../adr/0007-kid-safety-and-privacy.md)). The message "Bleh! That was plastic, not food." shows where the snack banner goes (X10). Under reduced motion the green tint shows, but not the wobble.
            - Queasy: for 3 seconds, the top speed is 60% of what it would be. No leg is lost, and protected time does not start. A Shell perk does not block it, because it is not a hit (A22). Tail Dash is not slowed: only the top speed drops, and the acceleration stays the same (A9, A10; open question 149).
            - No stacking: while the axolotl is queasy, another plastic worm does nothing, and the queasy timer does not restart.
            - Queasy time pauses with pause (A17) and while the banking card shows. It ends when the carry home starts (A11), on Go home, when the area is beaten, and when the trip ends.
            - Only the explorer gets queasy, never a following rescue (C3).
            - The spat-out worm lands 48 pixels ahead of the head, kept inside the area. If that point is inside a current or the Home Spot safe area, the worm stays where it was. It stays where it lands and can slow the axolotl again later. Every trip, the worms start back at their spawn places.
            - A plastic worm is never a snack. It never counts toward opening the exit (H2), and the Snack magnet never pulls it (A19).
            - Placement: every plastic worm starts outside the safe area, never under a lily pad, more than 140 pixels from every tilapia route, and at least 371.5 pixels from every current, so a queasy one-leg axolotl's queasy time ends before it can reach a current (A9). In every area, every snack starts at least 80 pixels from the edge of every hazard's shape (predator routes not included); the canal has no hazard with a shape, so its snacks do not change.
            - Fact card: "Plastic is not food" (C13), found the first time the axolotl comes within 100 pixels of a plastic worm, outside the Home Spot.
        - Ghost nets (built, issue #76; values in Tuning values, provisional): torn, loose, old fishing nets, anchored in place. A ghost net is not fishing line: lines stay with Area 10's lures. It snags the axolotl, which wriggles free, slowed for a moment. It only slows: it never hits, and it is not a threat for the risk ladder (B10).
            - Look: torn grey-green mesh with knotted edges, still in both motion modes. Its mesh pattern reads in greyscale on its own. The axolotl wriggles free and never looks trapped or hurt ([ADR 0007](../adr/0007-kid-safety-and-privacy.md)).
            - Touch: each net is a rectangle. The explorer is snagged when its body centre comes within the net's reach, 18 pixels, of the rectangle's edge, the edge included (open question 194).
            - Snagged: for 2 seconds, the top speed is 50% of what it would be, multiplied with the leg factor. No leg is lost, and protected time does not start. A Shell perk plays no part, because it is not a hit (A22). Tail Dash is not slowed: only the top speed drops, and the acceleration stays the same (A9, A10; open question 193).
            - On entry only, with no stacking: a snag starts only when the explorer swims into a net's reach from outside every net's reach, never again each step inside, and never while it is already snagged, so the snag timer does not restart. Entering overlapping nets at once counts as one entry.
            - The message "Snagged on an old net! Wriggle free." shows where the snack banner goes (X10). While snagged, a small snag cue shows over the explorer's head, in both motion modes. Under full motion the axolotl wriggles; under reduced motion it does not, and only the slowdown and the cue show.
            - Snag time pauses with pause (A17) and while the banking card shows. It ends when the carry home starts (A11), on Go home, when the area is beaten, and when the trip ends.
            - Only the explorer is snagged, never a following rescue (C3).
            - Placement: every ghost net sits outside the safe area, clear of the Home Spot ring and the exit, farther than a lunge from every tilapia route, and at least 190.5 pixels from every current, so a snag always ends before a snagged axolotl reaches a current. A snagged one-leg axolotl swims 45 pixels per second, which could not beat Area 4's current (A9; open question 195). Every snack starts at least 80 pixels from the edge of every net, by the hazard rule above.
            - Fact card: "Lost fishing nets" (C13), found the first time the axolotl comes within 100 pixels of a net's edge, outside the Home Spot.
            - Drifting ghost nets (built as a shared part, issue #117, opt-in per area; values in Tuning values, provisional; open question 258): a ghost net whose data has a drift moves slowly back and forth along a straight path from its place to its place plus the drift, slowing at both ends, on the trip's element clock, so it pauses with the game (A17) and starts again at 0 on each trip. A phase 2 area lays each drift path along the flow of the current nearby. A path is at most 200 pixels long, on a 16 second cycle: a 160 pixel path averages 20 pixels per second and peaks at about 31, well under a one-leg axolotl's 90. A net with no drift never moves. A drifting net snags like a still net, measured where it is now; it never costs a leg and is not a threat for the risk ladder (B10). It is met within 100 pixels of where it is now. Every placement rule uses its whole drift path: snack starts, mobile snack lanes and spots (H4), the gap measure, and the net rules above, 190.5 pixels from every current (A9), the predator clearances, the spots, the stranding places, and the exit. The mats' push-out checks each net where it is now. Under reduced motion it still drifts, because it is gameplay, with no extra sway; in greyscale it looks the same as a still net.
        - Moving hazards (built as a shared part, issue #117, opt-in per area; owner decision on issue #117, 2026-10-23; [ADR 0021](../adr/0021-danger-pass-chase-rule-and-moving-hazards.md); values in Tuning values, provisional; open questions 255 and 256): a hazard that moves on a fixed, deterministic cycle and costs one leg on touch. It is a danger part (N2, the danger pass), so it is outside the one-new-thing rule.
            - Type and looks: each type is data, with its timing, depth, shaft width, touch reach, meet range, fact card, and look. Looks are data too, so a later area can add a look, such as a pond look in Area 3 (issue #120). The first type is the trajinera punting pole: a trajinera's bright, painted bow on the surface over the pole, with no people, and the pole.
            - Cycle: a straight shaft comes down from the surface at its x. Each cycle, from its own offset, it rests up and out of the water, warns (still out of the water), jabs down to its depth at a steady speed, holds there, then lifts back up at a steady speed. At rest and during the warning it touches nothing. Its clock starts at 0 each trip and runs only on swimming steps, so it pauses with the game (A17), while the banking card shows, and during the carry home.
            - Telegraph (A2): the punting pole's warning lasts 1 second, at least the 0.8 second floor, and a type whose warning is shorter is refused. The boat's shadow darkens on the water under the bow, and the pole's tip dips to the surface with a ripple ring. In greyscale the shadow and the ring have an ink outline; under reduced motion they show still, and the jab still moves, because it is gameplay.
            - Touch: the axolotl's body centre within the touch reach of the shaft, from the surface to the tip, is a touch. A touch is one hit, costing a leg, by the shared hit rules (A4, A5, A11, A22), so it counts against Star 3 (H6); it is never a bump. Each step checks the predators, then the still hazards, then the moving hazards, and at most one hit lands per step. At the default 180 pixel depth, the punting pole's danger zone is x ± 23 pixels from the surface down to y 278, so in an area two screens tall everything below y 278 is safe from it; a spawn with a depth of its own reaches deeper.
            - It is not a predator, so it is outside the one-at-a-time rule (A1). Its fixed cycle is always on show. A phase 2 area keeps each sweep farther than every predator's lunge range from that predator's patrol loop.
            - Risk and placement: it is a threat for the risk ladder (B10), its shape its full sweep (the jab at full depth) and S its touch reach. Snack starts and mobile snacks keep 80 pixels from its sweep, and the gap measure keeps out of its sweep by its touch reach. It counts toward the danger bar (N2).
            - Never scary: the pole is never shown hitting or pinning the axolotl; a hit shows only the usual leg-loss poof ([ADR 0007](../adr/0007-kid-safety-and-privacy.md)).
            - Fact card: "Trajineras" (C13), found the first time the axolotl comes within 180 pixels of a pole's sweep, outside the Home Spot.
        - Pulsing current: a current that pulses on a cycle and can slow the axolotl.
        - Murky water: murky, polluted water. Staying in it too long costs a leg.
        - Trajinera boats: fishing boats, like Xochimilco's trajineras, with people fishing. Some snacks are lures on a fishing line, and eating a lure costs a leg. The line and the boat's shadow are always visible, so kids can learn to spot the trap. It is shown gently: the line tugs, the axolotl wriggles free, and a hook is never shown in the axolotl.
        - A hazard that costs a leg counts as a hit: A5 and A22 apply to it as they do to a tilapia's hit (owner decision, issue #73).
    - Timing elements:
        - Water hyacinth mats (built, issue #103; owner decisions on issue #103, 2026-10-22; values in Tuning values, provisional; [ADR 0020](../adr/0020-u-turn-areas-garter-snake-and-hyacinth-mats.md)): Area 5's one timing element, element type `water-hyacinth`. Each spawn is a pair of floating plant mats that slowly drift together to close a narrow channel, then drift apart to open it. The player crosses while the channel is open. Mats are not hazards and not threats for the risk ladder (B10).
            - Shape (open question 226): in the side view, each pair is one mat above and one mat below a narrow horizontal channel, as Area 4 draws the gap between two chinampas. The two mats drift together along y to close it. A pair spans its row's full height at its x, so a closed pair gates that row, and the way round is through the other row. This is the key reading for the owner to confirm; the other reading was both mats floating at the surface and drifting together along x.
            - Cycle, 8 seconds (open question 227): open for 3 seconds, the first 1 second of which the mats drift apart; then closing for 1 second, the mats visibly drifting together, which is the warning; then closed for 4 seconds. Pair 1 starts its cycle at 0 seconds and pair 2 at 4 seconds, so when one is open the other is closed. The channel counts as open once its clear height is at least 36 pixels, the safe passing width, and fully open it is 72 pixels tall. A channel is short enough that a one-leg axolotl that enters as it opens is through before closing starts: at most 189 pixels long with this tuning (Area 5's are 150).
            - Blocking (open question 228): each mat is solid at all times, at its position for the current time. The explorer's body centre stays out of each mat grown by 18 pixels, the net reach. Tail Dash and a bump do not pass through a mat. Only the explorer is blocked: the following rescued axolotl, the predators, the snacks, and the carry home are not, so every predator route and its reach, every snack start and darted snack landing, every hiding spot, stranding place, and net, and the exit stay clear of each mat pair's whole sweep, and nothing a player needs is ever inside a mat.
            - Never trapping: a mat never costs a leg, is never a hit, and never traps the axolotl. A mat that closes on the explorer pushes it out along the channel to the channel's nearer end, never into another mat, a net, a current, or out of the swim bounds; when the nearer end is not clear, it goes to the other end.
            - Mat time runs only on swimming steps, like snag time and queasy time, so it pauses with the game (A17) and while the banking card shows, and it starts again at 0 on each trip.
            - Reduced motion: the mats still move, because the movement is gameplay, but without sway. Greyscale: the mats have an outline.
            - Fact card: "Water hyacinth" (C13), found the first time the axolotl comes within 180 pixels of a mat pair's sweep, outside the Home Spot (open question 229).
    - Areas 11 and up: the carp, which stirs up mud so there is less to see (the common carp itself ships earlier, as a predator, issue #117, above); the sharp can or broken bottle, which costs a leg when touched; and gates. New habitats also wait for areas 11 and up.
4. **N4 Feeding the tank** (issue #33). The player brings snacks home and feeds the axolotls in the tank. Happy axolotls give a small, capped bonus on the next trip, such as a hint sparkle. There is no penalty for not feeding. Axolotls are never sad or sick.
5. **N5 Wearable sets** (issue #36). Examples: unicorn (horn, shirt, socks, and hooves), shark, astronaut, pirate, dinosaur, deep-sea diver, and superhero. A set covers 4 to 8 of the 8 slots. Pieces are found one at a time across trips. There are three kinds of set:
    - Common sets: quick, good first goals.
    - Single-rarity sets: fully Rare, fully Epic, or fully Legendary.
    - Mixed sets: usually one rare piece is the hardest to find.

    The full set unlocks a mild themed ability, a visual flourish, and a special pose with extra cheers in Tank Top Model (N1). The ability is separate from perk caps, but small, and a little stronger for rarer sets. Removing any piece turns these off. Sets are data. Each set has a Collection Book page that shows its found and missing pieces. The MVP prepares for sets with the optional set ID (Implementation Decision 12).

6. **N6 Background music** (epic issue #150, under issue #147; issues #151 to #153). Cozy but fun music, made live in code (M1 to M12, [ADR 0025](../adr/0025-procedural-background-music.md), [music guide](../music-guide.md)). The Home side of the game has one cue, Sunny Tank, cozy but happy. Each habitat has its own cue: Canal Paddle for the canal, Lily Pad Hop for the lily pad pond, and Garden Boats for the chinampa gardens. Every new habitat adds its own cue (M3). A dusk or night area plays the dusk version of its habitat's cue (the dusk rule, M4), so it never needs new music. Music never reacts to danger (M5). One music switch turns it on and off (M8). Sound effects are not part of N6; they come later under issue #147. The approved sketches are in `prototypes/music/`. Built (issues #152 and #153): the music engine and synth, all four cues, the shared dusk rule with a dusk version of each habitat cue, and the music switch on the title screen, in the Home Tank and on the trip pause screen. A trip plays its area's habitat cue, cross-fading from Sunny Tank and back to it on the way home; Area 3, whose `'pond-dusk'` look is dusk, plays Lily Pad Hop (dusk). Provisional choices are open questions 421 to 424.

7. **N7 Sound effects** (epic issue #162, under issue #147; issues #163 to #167). A sound for the moments a player cares about, made live in code on the music's audio engine (S1 to S16, [ADR 0026](../adr/0026-procedural-sound-effects.md), [sound guide](../sound-guide.md)). Sounds are watery and wooden, in the key of the cue playing, and all about as loud as each other. Wins end on the home chord; a lost leg is a kind "uh-oh"; a predator warning is one soft ripple; being carried home is a lullaby; and constant things stay silent. A sounds switch, separate from the music switch, is on at first launch and kept on the device. Built in three slices: the engine, the switch and the 12 sounds a player hears every trip (snacks, finds, Tail Dash, a lost leg and its regrowth, banking, the exit, stars, and taps); then hazards and rescue; then the Home Tank and menus. Built (issue #167): the Home Tank, book and menu sounds, 12 in all: back in the Home Tank on the tank arrival card, picking up and placing a Decoration, putting on and taking off a Wearable, putting on a Dream colour, opening a fact card, turning a Collection Book or Treasure Chest page, a refuge milestone, a new area on the area map, the close or back tap, and Surprise me. Built (issue #165): the first slice's trip sounds, the snack ladder and the last 3 snacks, finds by rarity one at a time, Tail Dash, the "uh-oh" and the leg back, banking by count, the exit's "ta-da", and the win with its stars on the tank arrival card, picked by pure rules and played by a sound router made for each trip. Built (issue #166): the hazard and rescue sounds, 16 in all: the warning ripple for a predator's warning and for a punting pole's warning on screen, the Shell block, the lullaby when carried home, the plastic worm "bleh", the ghost net snag and the wriggle free when it ends, the closed exit, the hyacinth mats opening on screen, the catch for a tadpole or shrimp on the snack ladder, the shrimp flick, the Paint shell after its find, Dash ready, the wobble of a sealed Dash pot and the break of one, and meeting a stranded axolotl and its safe sound after the bank, with the hazard moments that have no trip event found each frame by a pure rule and played by the trip's sound router. Provisional choices are open questions 446 to 457 (issue #167), 463 to 478 (issue #165) and 480 to 497 (issue #166).

## Further Notes

### Long-term world (vision, not MVP)

Direction from the project owner: in time, the setting gains much more depth.
It becomes several connected habitats that a player swims between during one trip.

Habitats are real freshwater places only, until the much later areas in the design note below. Axolotls live only in fresh water, so there are no coral reefs and no "magic reef" for now.
Candidate habitats:

- Xochimilco canals.
- Cenotes and underground caves (dark; a fit for Glow Gills).
- Spring-fed pools (clear and full of plants).
- Lily-pad forests and chinampa floating gardens.
- Sunken ruins (old canals and Aztec stones).
- A deep lake bed (Lake Xochimilco or Lake Chalco).

Each habitat can have its own look, hazards, treasures, and fun facts.
The MVP keeps one canal area but must not block this (see X9 and Implementation Decision 8).
The area chain and refuge goal (N2) are the first steps.

### Later areas beyond realism (design note)

Direction from the project owner, 2026-10-20 (issue #32).
Much later areas may drift from realism, for example to a volcano, space, land, or fairytale and fantasy places.
Predators and hazards change with the landscape too, for example aliens in space areas and fairytale creatures in fantasy areas.
The N3 guardrails still apply.
Fantasy areas and imaginary creatures are labelled as imaginary, like Dream colours are labelled "not found in nature".
Fact cards stay true to real animals and real places.
Areas 1 to 10 stay in real Xochimilco habitats.

### Rarity glow (decision)

Owner request (issue #68): a treasure has a faint glow in its rarity's colour, so a player can tell its rarity at a glance.
The glow is about as faint as the GFP halo ([art style guide](../art-style-guide.md) 3c). It never pulses faster than once every 2.4 seconds, and it is static under reduced motion.
It shows on owned and revealed treasures: in the area once revealed (B8), on the find toast, the banking card, and the tank arrival card, in the Treasure Chest, and on placed Decorations. The Trip Bag panel shows no treasure icons, so the find toast and the banking card carry the glow for its finds. Paint shells and Dream colour swatches glow by their own rarity too. Open questions 85 and 86 list every place it shows, and where it does not.
Colour stays the weakest cue. The rarity badge shape and name still carry rarity (B2, X2), and the glow never replaces them.
Product owner decisions, 2026-10-20:

- Common becomes green everywhere, for the glow and the Common badge ([art style guide](../art-style-guide.md) 3e). It stays clearly apart from the reed green (`#86e660`) and the GFP halo (`#7dff8f`) in the canal.
- Legendary keeps gold (`#ffc12e`).
- No glow while a treasure is hidden. The glow appears once it is revealed (B8), and the hint sparkle (B7) stays the only cue for a hidden treasure.

Built in 0.20.0 (PR #85): B2, B7, B8, C11, C15, the Testing Decisions and open questions 83 to 88 hold the built rules.

### Treasure checklist (decision)

Owner request (issue #69): the player can see every treasure, so they know what they have and what they still need to find.
The Treasure Chest shows every catalogue treasure, found or not, in catalogue order within each section. A found treasure shows as today (C11).
An unfound treasure shows a flat silhouette of its icon, in the style of the unfound-morph silhouette (C12), with a "?" and its rarity badge and rarity name. An unfound Dream colour's silhouette is a plain swatch.
A silhouette fills in once its treasure is banked. "X of Y found" stays. Found and unfound are never told apart by colour alone (X2).
Issue #69's recommendations: it lives in the Treasure Chest, not a new screen; an unfound treasure's name shows as "???", with a hint by rarity based on B6; and an unfound Wearable shows its slot but not its perk.
Built in 0.20.0 (PR #85): C11 holds the built rules; open questions 89 to 92 track the owner's confirmation.
Owner decisions on issue #106 (2026-10-22): an unfound treasure's hint names its area first, then the rarity hint, also for areas not yet unlocked; found treasures are unchanged. C11 holds the built rules; open questions 188 to 190 track the owner's confirmation.

### Rarer treasures nearer hazards (decision)

Owner request (issue #70): rarer treasures are harder to get, as well as harder to find, by sitting near a hazard (in area 1, the tilapia). The risk ladder below says how near each rarity's spots sit.
Each hiding spot gets a pure, tested risk measure, for example its distance from each tilapia's patrol route and lunge range. Placement picks spots by both how well hidden and how risky they are for the rarity.
Product owner decisions, 2026-10-20:

- Risk ladder. In each area, each rarity's hiding spots sit in one band of the risk measure, one row per rarity in the table below, listed from least to most risky. A spot is riskier the nearer it is to the nearest tilapia patrol route. Lunge range is the distance within which a tilapia starts a lunge (A3), and a spot is within lunge range when it is that close to any point of a tilapia's patrol route. The bands never overlap, and each band is strictly riskier than the row above it, checked by a test between adjacent rows.

    | Rarity | Risk band |
    |---|---|
    | Common | Never within lunge range, in the band furthest from the route. |
    | Rare | Outside lunge range, in the band closest to the route (near the edge of a patrol). |
    | Epic | Within lunge range, short of its deepest part, so grabbing one means watching the telegraph. |
    | Legendary | Within lunge range, in its deepest part, closest to the route, so grabbing one means watching the telegraph. It also needs Tail Dash, as B6 says. |

- Fairness. Every treasure can always be grabbed without being hit, by timing or Tail Dash. No spot sits where a tilapia rests or turns, so a skilled player never loses a leg by force. It stays kid-safe and never scary.
- No change to the hint sparkle (B7) or to the rarity glow decisions (issue #68).
- If too few spots meet a rarity's risk rule, more spots are added as area data.

This ladder was first defined for the tilapia only.
The area framework (N3, issue #73) makes it generic, as B10 says. Every predator route and every hazard that costs a leg is a threat with a strike range S: a predator's lunge range, or a hazard's reach from its shape's edge. Its bands are Common farther than S + 60 pixels, Rare more than S and at most S + 60, Epic more than S / 2 and at most S, and Legendary at most S / 2. For the tilapia, S is 140 pixels, so the bands stay 200, 140, and 70 pixels, and the canal's spots and placements do not change. A spot's band is the riskiest band any threat puts it in, and spot weights use the shortest distance to any threat. Hazards that only slow (such as plastic worms, ghost nets, and currents) are not threats, and an area with no threat uses B6 alone. See open questions 120 and 123.
Built in 0.19.0 (PR #84): B6 and B10, the Testing Decisions, the Tuning values, and CONTEXT.md (Hiding spot) hold the built rules. The owner approved re-laying the canal so the bands can hold (issue #70).

### Shuffled hiding spots (decision)

Product owner decision, 2026-10-18 (issue #11): hiding spots shuffle each trip.
This replaces the fixed spot per treasure that B4 used to describe.
Each trip picks a few unbanked treasures (issue #39), then a spot for each from that treasure's pool (B4, B6).
Spot ids stay stable per area (X9, Implementation Decision 2).
The trip seed that picks the treasures and spots is never saved. The save (issue #15) keeps banked ids only.
Lost treasure ids are kept for this play session only and are never saved.
The same decision sets at least 3 tilapia in the canal (A1).

### Open questions (recommended defaults)

| # | Question | Recommended default |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Does only a lunge cost a leg, or any touch? | Only lunges. |
| 2 | Do legs regrow one at a time, or all at once? | One at a time. |
| 4 | What does the Home Spot look like: a burrow or a buoy? | Art decision. |
| 5 | Do strandings stop after all morphs are rescued? | Yes, none after that. The 40% chance per trip (C2, open question 138; issue #111) does not change this: only morphs not yet owned are stranded, so there are at most 7 rescues (issue #94). |
| 6 | Do snacks change in the MVP? | Decided: yes. Snacks speed up regrowth while a leg is missing (A19), and every snack must be eaten to open the exit (H2). |
| 7 | Closing the game mid-trip sends unbanked finds back into the canal (they can turn up again on a later trip, in fresh spots). Is that acceptable? | Yes. Nothing mid-trip is saved (D2, D3): the game reopens in the Home Tank, eaten snacks start over, and a stranded axolotl left waiting is not kept (open question 56). Treasures already banked at the Home Spot stay saved (open question 57). Needs confirmation. |
| 8 | Does the update check need its own ADR? | Yes, before slice E, if it makes any request beyond loading the game. [ADR 0007](../adr/0007-kid-safety-and-privacy.md) allows no third-party requests; AGENTS.md says no network requests. |
| 9 | What are the exact perk values and caps? | Tuning. Start from the Tuning values below. Built with those values (issue #16): every cap, the Dash perk as a longer Tail Dash (open question 70), and a fixed Snack magnet pull speed (open question 71). Tune after play-testing. |
| 10 | Can a Dream colour be combined with spots? | Decided: yes. Spots draw on top of the Dream colour (F14). |
| 11 | Can one owned Dream colour be on more than one axolotl at the same time? | Decided: yes. It is a paint, not a single item (F14). |
| 12 | Is the explorer shown with its own look and outfit on trips? | Decided: yes (F7). |
| 13 | Which treasure is an area's special treasure, and does it count if banked on an earlier trip? | One treasure per area is marked special in the area data. Needs confirmation. |
| 14 | Do eaten snacks stay eaten after going home by choice, or after closing the game? | They stay eaten until carried home or the area is beaten. Not saved (D2, open question 56), so closing the game starts them over. Needs confirmation. |
| 15 | Is the full reset when carried home (A12) too harsh? | Decided for now: full reset. Review after play-tests. Fallback: a softer, partial reset. |

| 17 | Where does the player go after the "Area beaten" screen? | The Home Tank. Needs confirmation. |

Spots stay off the head and never look like morph marks. The Spots step's picture buttons show the body and tail up close (F3). Needs confirmation. |
| 20 | Which suggested names are on the list (F17)? | Replaced by open question 33: the list is now split into boy names, girl names, and names for anyone. |

Anything else is dropped and does not count. It never shows in the name field: it is dropped as it is typed or pasted. Needs confirmation. |

They need 20 pixels at 460 wide and 320 to 360 tall (the snack banner always, the hint while Go home shows) and at 480 wide and 320 to 360 tall (both, while Go home shows). Only the "Tie-dye galaxy Paint shell" and "Rainbow dragonfly wings" toasts wait, at 460 x 320 and 460 x 340 with Go home showing (open question 154); this does not happen in play: the Trip Bag is banked and the toast cleared. With two or three messages up, a lower-priority banner uses 20 pixels more often on narrow or short screens. At 460 x 360, 480 x 320 to 360, 320 x 440, 390 x 440, 667 x 375, 844 x 360, and 1024 x 360 nothing waits. The toast sits over the status panels at the top-left, above the middle, at 460 x 320; over the panels from 79.2 pixels down at 320 x 440; and at the top edge at 800 x 600 and 1024 x 768. The middle rule is confirmed by the owner (option (c), chosen in review). Measuring the middle on the water drawn on screen needs confirmation, as does the rest. |

A find: the 32 pixel treasure icon, the name in bold 20 pixel text, then the 24 pixel rarity badge and "<Rarity> <Category>" in bold 18 pixel text (for example "Rare Wearable"). A rescue: the 32 pixel portrait, the morph name (for example "Wild axolotl"), then the 24 pixel morph badge and "Following you!". Badge shape and name, never colour alone. Needs confirmation. |
| 40 | What happens when finds come quickly (B8)? | The newest find replaces the toast at once, at full opacity, and the 2 second hold starts again. While the toast waits for room or the game is paused, no time passes for it, in any phase. Needs confirmation. |

Measured, each shown alone: both show on every supported screen. The snack banner needs 20 pixels at 460 wide and 320 to 360 tall; the hint needs it there while Go home shows; both need it at 480 wide and 320 to 360 tall while Go home shows. Elsewhere both show at 24 pixels. With two or three messages up, the toast takes its place first, then the snack banner, then the hint; a banner uses 20 pixels more often then, and waits when no place is left for it even at 20. Needs confirmation. |

Needs confirmation. |
| 47 | How does the Dream colour tab fit the smallest canvases, 460 x 320 and 320 x 440 (F6, F15)? | Its labels never hide (fit step 2 does not apply), so every Dream colour keeps its name. After the other fit steps, it shows fewer picture buttons per page. Measured with all 13 canal options: 2 per page at 460 x 320 and at 320 x 440, 6 at 375 x 667, and 8 at 844 x 390 and 1280 x 800. With Lily green (issue #75) and Firefly gold (issue #98), all 15 options show 2 per page, in 8 pages (the last with 1), at 460 x 320 and 320 x 440, and 8 then 7 where 8 fit. With Poppy red (issue #76), all 16 options show 2 per page, in 8 pages, at 460 x 320 and 320 x 440, and 8 then 8 where 8 fit. With Plum (issue #103), all 17 options show 2 per page, in 9 pages (the last with 1), at 460 x 320 and 320 x 440, and 8, 8, then 1 where 8 fit. At 460 x 320 and 320 x 440, 3 or more options give 2 per page with More colours. Needs confirmation. |
| 48 | What does the Dream colour tab show when no Dream colour is owned (F6)? | Only "Real colour", with the helper line "Find Paint shells in the canal to get Dream colours!". Needs confirmation. |
| 49 | Does "Surprise me" change the Dream colour (F6)? | No. "Surprise me" never changes the Dream colour, like the colour and the boy or girl. Needs confirmation. |
| 50 | Where does the Iridescent sheen move (F15, F16)? | It glides along the axolotl in the Home Tank, the restyle preview, and on trips. Under reduced motion and on picture buttons it stays still at x -10 ([art style guide](../art-style-guide.md) 6p). Needs confirmation. |
| 51 | Where does "Start over" go (D7)? | A secondary button on the title screen, under Start, shown only once an axolotl is owned. Not in the Home Tank hub: a fifth hub tile does not fit the smallest canvas, 460 x 320 (open question 25). Tab reaches it, and Enter and Space still press Start. It hides while the update prompt shows (E1). Awaiting owner confirmation. |
| 52 | What are the two steps of "Start over", and their words (D7)? | Step 1: the title "Start over?", the text "This erases your axolotls, treasures, tank and stars on this device.", and the buttons "Keep playing" (focused first) and "Start over". Step 2: the title "Are you sure?", the text "This can't be undone.", and the buttons "No, keep them" (focused first) and "Yes, erase". Escape keeps everything on either step. Each card appears and leaves at once, with no motion. On the smallest canvases the step 1 text shortens to "This erases everything.", and the title hides when a card still does not fit. The go button takes no input for the first 0.8 seconds a card shows, so a double tap cannot erase. Awaiting owner confirmation. |
| 53 | Where is the note for parents (D8)? | "Grown-ups: progress is saved only in this browser on this device. Clearing browser data erases it." It is on the step 1 card of "Start over" and in the README. Awaiting owner confirmation. |
| 54 | What happens with an unreadable save (D5)? | No message. The game starts fresh. A damaged save's text is kept under `glam-gills:save-unreadable` until the next "Start over"; if one is already kept, the first one stays. A save from a newer version is left untouched, and saving is off for that page session, so it is never overwritten. That build cannot erase it: the game starts fresh with no axolotl owned, so "Start over" does not show (D7), and there is no way back to the title. Progress made on that build is lost when the page closes. Whether an older build should offer a way to erase a newer save is the owner's call ([review thread](https://github.com/brenpike/glam-gills/pull/66#discussion_r4326822771)). Awaiting owner confirmation. |
| 55 | What if the game is open in two tabs? | The last save wins. Tabs do not sync. Awaiting owner confirmation. |
| 56 | What is not saved (D2)? | Eaten snacks and a stranded axolotl left waiting, as well as the trip itself: the Trip Bag, the position, leg state, Shell blocks used, and the trip seed. Reopening the game starts the snacks over, and the next trip picks its stranded axolotl afresh. Awaiting owner confirmation. |
| 57 | When does banking mid-trip save (D1)? | Treasures banked at the Home Spot mid-trip save at once, so closing the game keeps them. A rescue banked mid-trip saves when the trip ends.
